I originally\n\u003E started with a simple DQN, added all improvements from the Rainbow paper, and\n\u003E finally changed C51 for a quantile regression (and plan to swap it for an\n\u003E Implicit Quantile Network).\n\u003E \n\u003E After implementing C51 (which was my first time with distributional RL) I\n\u003E started playing with policies that take advantage of the learned distributions :\n\u003E By independently taking `N` samples from each action-value distribution, scoring\n\u003E actions by averaging the samples, and picking the greedy action with respect to\n\u003E these scores, I was able to make the agent learn faster than similar agents\n\u003E using only NoisyNets or an epsilon-greedy policy (I'm still using NoisyNet, this\n\u003E is done on top of it). In the limiting cases, `N=1` is just Thompson Sampling\n\u003E and `N=+Infinity` is just a plain greedy policy.\n\u003E \n\u003E Finding an optimal value for `N` proved to be a challenge, so I decided to pick\n\u003E a random value for it at the start of each episode (`N = 2**rng.uniform(8,12)`\n\u003E for a QR-DQN with 32 quantiles\u002Faction works well in my experiments), which led\n\u003E to even better results.\n\u003E \n\u003E I later found out about\n\u003E [DLTV](https:\u002F\u002Fproceedings.mlr.press\u002Fv97\u002Fmavrin19a\u002Fmavrin19a.pdf) which made the\n\u003E agent discover new behaviors, but performed worse than previous experiments\n\u003E overall. Inspired by it, I tried something I did not find in previous works and\n\u003E got the best results out of all my previous experiments :\n\u003E \n\u003E At each time step, compute an `exploration_score` as the ratio of \"intra-action\n\u003E variance\" over \"inter-action variance\"\n\u003E ([rendered latex equation](https:\u002F\u002Fpierre-couy.dev\u002Fmedia\u002Fext\u002Fdrl_exploration_score_eqn.png)).\n\u003E I then take `N\u002Fexploration_score` samples from each distribution, and pick an\n\u003E action as described above. (more details at the end of this post)\n\u003E \n\u003E For anyone reading this, I have a few questions :\n\u003E \n\u003E 1. Are you aware of any previous work I missed that tries similar exploration\n\u003E    policies with distributional RL (interpolating between Thompson sampling and\n\u003E    the greedy policy)\n\u003E 2. Most papers I found about learning from multiple exploration policies seem to\n\u003E    be in the context of multi-actor parallelization. Is there any novelty in\n\u003E    randomizing the policy parameters at the start of each episode, especially in\n\u003E    the single-actor case ?\n\u003E 3. Is any part of what I'm doing worth the time it would take to quantitatively\n\u003E    evaluate it ? I've been doing it mainly for learning and fun and have only\n\u003E    qualitatively evaluated it so far. However, if there's a chance I can\n\u003E    contribute to the field, I'll gladly make some time to compare it to\n\u003E    published papers on ALE.\n\u003E \n\u003E -------------------------\n\u003E \n\u003E #### A few more details\n\u003E \n\u003E I actually track a moving average and standard deviation of the exploration\n\u003E score, which lets me shift\u002Frescale its values to a target average and standard\n\u003E deviation, and divide N by the shifted\u002Frescaled value. I initially started with\n\u003E a target average of 1 and standard deviation of 1 as well (which gave good\n\u003E results), then tried randomizing these parameters at the start of each episode\n\u003E as well. This led to a lot more diversity in the policies and even better\n\u003E results.\n\u003E \n\u003E Since this worked so well, I additionally randomized the noise strength in the\n\u003E NoisyNet layers.\n\u003E \n\u003E Overall, this made the agent a lot more robust to deviating from what it\n\u003E considers to be the optimal trajectory, and allowed it to learn complex\n\u003E behaviors previous iterations were never able to learn (e.g. taking a few steps\n\u003E back to gain momentum, waiting for good cycles, or dodging hammer bros)\n\u003E \n\u003E ------------------------\n\u003E \n\u003E #### Watch it learn\n\u003E \n\u003E For anyone interested, I made a\n\u003E [live stream of the training in progress](https:\u002F\u002Ftwitch.tv\u002Fpcouy_) with graphs\n\u003E and some more details on the experiments I'm running. The current training run\n\u003E was started ~2.5 days ago. The agent has finished and unlocked levels up to 5-1, and is currently learning 5-2.\n\u003E \n\u003E -----------------------\n\u003E \n\u003E #### A lot more details\n\u003E \n\u003E ::: spoiler Long text hidden, click to expand\n\u003E **Available actions :** The agent does not have access to the up and down\n\u003E buttons, the available actions only use left, right, A and B.\n\u003E \n\u003E Adding the down button would double the total number of actions (because down\n\u003E can be pressed on top of all available actions).\n\u003E \n\u003E **Reward function :** It mainly consists of\n\u003E `reward(t) = max(0, x(t) - previous_best_x)` \\+ a larger reward for beating a\n\u003E stage. I had to tweak the scaling of both components.\n\u003E \n\u003E I initially had penalties for time and death, but one made the agent suicidal in\n\u003E front of hard-to-overcome obstacles, while the other made it fear them too much\n\u003E and hug the left side of the screen. Removing both proved to increase the\n\u003E performance.\n\u003E \n\u003E One trick that seems to help with most '\\*-3' levels (which have a lot of void\n\u003E to fall into) was to hold the reward while the vertical velocity of Mario is\n\u003E negative (meaning it is falling). Without this trick, the agent would sometimes\n\u003E get stuck learning to jump the farthest it can into the void.\n\u003E \n\u003E **Stage scheduling :** Each episode is one attempt on one level. At the start of\n\u003E each episode, a stage is randomly picked with probability proportional to\n\u003E `1\u002F(number of times the stage was beaten)` among the unlocked stages. Each stage\n\u003E is unlocked after the previous one has been beaten 30 times, with only 1-1\n\u003E unlocked at the start of the training.\n\u003E \n\u003E **Available stages :** The first iterations of the agent were unable to learn\n\u003E maze castles (4-3, 7-3 and 8-4), so I removed them all. The reward function will\n\u003E give rewards for the first path the agent tries, then the agent will be\n\u003E teleported back by the game and no reward is received until it finds the right\n\u003E path and gets past the point where the game teleported it back. I plan to test\n\u003E newer (better) versions of the agent on these stages only and see if mazes can\n\u003E be re-added to the pool.\n\u003E \n\u003E I've also removed underwater stages (2-2 and 7-2). The agent can learn them\n\u003E fine, but the game dynamics are really different from all other stages and\n\u003E they're really boring to watch. Since I already removed a bunch of stages, I\n\u003E figured I could remove these as well but I may re-add them with mazes because\n\u003E beating every level is cooler than beating a cherry-picked selection.\n\u003E \n\u003E Since 8-4 is the only stage that requires going down a pipe, I considered it was\n\u003E not worth it to add the down action and will likely never re-add it to the pool,\n\u003E which would unfortunately be really anti-climactic...\n\u003E \n\u003E **Replay buffer warm-up :** After initially using the standard approach of\n\u003E filling the buffer with transitions sampled from a random policy before training\n\u003E the neural net, I came-up with a \"soft warm-up\" scheme in which the first\n\u003E gradient updates happen after only 2000 transitions, but initially happen every\n\u003E few thousand transitions and gradually become more frequent until the replay\n\u003E buffer is full. Together with my custom exploration policy, this works very well\n\u003E : the agent very quickly starts behaving similar to a \"right + random button\"\n\u003E policy before learning to actually jump and run.\n\u003E \n\u003E **Custom n-step bootstrapping :** When I initially implemented n-step bootstrap\n\u003E targets, I initially used `n=3` from the Rainbow paper, noting the same\n\u003E instabilities as the paper did for higher n values. I then found\n\u003E [the Retrace(\\lambda) paper](https:\u002F\u002Farxiv.org\u002Fabs\u002F1606.02647) which seems to\n\u003E successfully address this by increasing `n` until the online network disagrees\n\u003E with the action choice from a stored transition. This makes n larger where the\n\u003E replay buffer data is on-policy, and smaller when it becomes off-policy. Since\n\u003E my GPU is already maxed and the training is already slow (20.8t\u002Fs when real-time\n\u003E is 20t\u002Fs) I could not afford the additional computations (building a training\n\u003E sample `(s(t), a(t), sum(r(t+0..n)), s(t+n))` needs up to `n_max` transitions to\n\u003E go through the online network).\n\u003E \n\u003E I'm trying to achieve similar sample efficiency gains by using cheaper\n\u003E alternatives as proxies for \"how off-policy is a given transition\" : I'm using\n\u003E the number of times a transition has been sampled, with\n\u003E `n = int(max(n_min, n_max * k**times_sampled)) ; 0\u003Ck\u003C1`. The currently running\n\u003E experiment uses `n_max=14`, `n_min=1` and `k=1\u002F1.3`. I'm pretty sure it helps\n\u003E early in the training, and it does not collapse like a constant `n=14` does\n\u003E \n\u003E **Stream setup :** As I said, this is something I do for my own fun, and I\n\u003E really wanted to be able to see the agent learn in real time. The code runs a\n\u003E separate process, to which frames from training episodes are sent in a queue.\n\u003E The process then sends the frames as raw RGB24 to an local UDP socket, to which\n\u003E [GStreamer](https:\u002F\u002Fgstreamer.freedesktop.org\u002F) connects and encodes the stream.\n\u003E With a simple [MediaMTX](https:\u002F\u002Fmediamtx.org\u002F) configuration, I can manage the\n\u003E Gstreamer process and have the stream available through WebRTC on my LAN.\n\u003E \n\u003E Then I figured someone else might have fun watching this, so I added a line to\n\u003E my MediaMTX config to send the stream to twitch and youtube. The overlay is a\n\u003E headless browser displaying custom HTML\u002FJS (using d3.js for the graphs) piping\n\u003E raw frames to [ffmpeg](https:\u002F\u002Fffmpeg.org\u002F). GStreamer handles compositing the\n\u003E two streams together into the side-by-side view.\n\u003E \n\u003E :::\n\u003E ","creator_id":64433,"community_id":10086,"removed":false,"locked":false,"published":"2026-06-03T15:54:26.240257Z","deleted":false,"nsfw":false,"embed_title":"pcouy_ - AI learned very precise controls in Super Mario Bros - 8-2 finish","embed_description":"Watch pcouy_'s clip titled \"AI learned very precise controls in Super Mario Bros - 8-2 finish\"","thumbnail_url":"https:\u002F\u002Flemmy.world\u002Fpictrs\u002Fimage\u002Ff800c0c7-48ed-4560-adb9-2f68d16173c6.jpeg","ap_id":"https:\u002F\u002Flemmy.pierre-couy.fr\u002Fpost\u002F2152282","local":false,"embed_video_url":"https:\u002F\u002Fclips.twitch.tv\u002Fembed?clip=DepressedMagnificentLemurSoonerLater-_w8Q6QIqm6-U-BXZ&parent=meta.tag","language_id":37,"featured_community":false,"featured_local":false,"url_content_type":"text\u002Fhtml"},"creator":{"id":64433,"name":"pcouy","banned":false,"published":"2023-06-12T07:39:16.861075Z","actor_id":"https:\u002F\u002Flemmy.pierre-couy.fr\u002Fu\u002Fpcouy","local":false,"deleted":false,"matrix_user_id":"@pcouy:matrix.pierre-couy.fr","bot_account":false,"instance_id":1051},"community":{"id":10086,"name":"machinelearning","title":"Machine Learning | Artificial Intelligence","description":"Welcome to Machine Learning – a versatile digital hub where Artificial Intelligence enthusiasts unite. While test-time computation offers a path to enhanced performance, most approaches treat reasoning as a fixed or heuristic process. In this work, we propose a new paradigm: viewing the model's internal reasoning, delimited by \u003Cthink\u003E and \u003C\u002Fthink\u003E tags, as a dynamic task vector machine. Rather than treating the content inside these tags as a mere trace of thought, we interpret the generation process itself as a mechanism through which the model \\textbf{constructs and refines its own task vectors} on the fly. We developed a method to optimize this dynamic task vector machine through RLVR and successfully trained an agentic web-search model. We present Lucy, a 1.7B-parameter SLM that leverages this dynamic reasoning mechanism with MCP integration to achieve 78.3% accuracy on the SimpleQA benchmark, performing on par with much larger models such as DeepSeek-V3.
